Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is happening in my head at the same time, all the time. My marriage, whether I’m messing things up at work or falling behind, bills, stuff around the house that I keep starting and then dropping, and then I start thinking maybe I’m just failing in general — as a husband, as a father, as the person who’s supposed to keep things together.

I work with computers, so I’m used to juggling details, but lately it feels like I’m juggling my whole life and I’m dropping pieces everywhere. Yeah. Very hard. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because I can sometimes see that I’m doing it — like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on the code in front of you,” or if I’m exercising I’ll try to just breathe and count reps or whatever — and it doesn’t really stop.

I’ll start with something small, like maybe I’m behind on a task or I didn’t explain something clearly at work, and then within a few minutes I’m twenty steps ahead. I’m imagining getting a bad review, then losing my job, then not being able to support my family, and then it becomes this whole thing where I’m the reason everything falls apart. And I know, logically, that I jumped way ahead, but knowing that doesn’t make it shut off. It feels like once it gets going, I’m just along for the ride.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.

It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.

I’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.

So… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.

My marriage, whether I’m messing things up at work or falling behind, bills, stuff around the house that I keep starting and then dropping, and then I start thinking maybe I’m just failing in general — as a husband, as a father, as the person who’s supposed to keep things together.\n\nIt’s not one specific thing, really. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for what I’ve forgotten or what’s about to go wrong, and then it lands on something else before I can even deal with the first thing. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because I can sometimes see that I’m doing it — like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on the code in front of you,” or if I’m exercising I’ll try to just breathe and count reps or whatever — and it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like one concern catches onto another one. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.

But the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.

So… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.

Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.

I’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.

That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because I can sometimes see that I’m doing it — like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on the code in front of you,” or if I’m exercising I’ll try to just breathe and count reps or whatever — and it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like one concern catches onto another one. I’ll start with something small, like maybe I’m behind on a task or I didn’t explain something clearly at work, and then within a few minutes I’m twenty steps ahead. I’m imagining getting a bad review, then losing my job, then not being able to support my family, and then it becomes this whole thing where I’m the reason everything falls apart. And I know, logically, that I jumped way ahead, but knowing that doesn’t make it shut off. It feels like once it gets going, I’m just along for the ride.\n\nDoctor: Before these worries became a problem, did you already have trouble with focus or finishing tasks going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. I got through school, and later college and grad school, so… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate what was just normal kid behavior from something meaningful.\n\nIf anything, the trouble now feels tied up with all the worrying and second-guessing. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. More like overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is excessive compared with the actual problems going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.\n\nBut the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.

Like, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.

That’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.

I’m imagining getting a bad review, then losing my job, then not being able to support my family, and then it becomes this whole thing where I’m the reason everything falls apart. And I know, logically, that I jumped way ahead, but knowing that doesn’t make it shut off. It feels like once it gets going, I’m just along for the ride.\n\nDoctor: Before these worries became a problem, did you already have trouble with focus or finishing tasks going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. I got through school, and later college and grad school, so… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate what was just normal kid behavior from something meaningful.\n\nIf anything, the trouble now feels tied up with all the worrying and second-guessing. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. More like overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is excessive compared with the actual problems going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.\n\nBut the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\nDoctor: Even when things are relatively calm, do you still find yourself worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.\n\nLike, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Very much so. I don’t really feel like I relax anymore in the normal sense of the word.

It’s like my body is wound tight almost all day. Even if I’m just sitting at my computer or I’m between tasks, my shoulders are up and locked, and I’ll realize I’m sort of bracing, like something is about to happen. Sometimes I don’t even notice it until I let out a breath or my neck starts hurting and I’m like, okay, I’ve apparently been sitting here like I’m waiting for bad news.

And sitting still is hard. Between meetings, I’ll get up and pace around, not even going anywhere useful, just back and forth. Or if I’m at chess club, which should be quiet and focused, I’ll catch myself tapping pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there calmly feels almost impossible. It’s embarrassing because I’m aware of it, but I can’t seem to settle my body down.

So yeah, I have a lot of trouble relaxing. Even when I’m supposed to be doing nothing, I feel like I’m still on alert.

Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about nine months now, give or take.

It really seemed to start when the cloud migration at work ramped up. I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think it’s that I’ve lost interest exactly. I still know, in my head, what kinds of things I usually like or want to do.

It’s more that I don’t always feel like I can fully get into them, if that makes sense. Like if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still show up for it or start doing it, but I’m not necessarily relaxed enough to really enjoy it the way I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same as losing pleasure, or if it’s just me being distracted and tense.

So… I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped caring about the things I enjoy. I just don’t feel as present with them lately.

But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. More like overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is excessive compared with the actual problems going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.\n\nBut the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\nDoctor: Even when things are relatively calm, do you still find yourself worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.\n\nLike, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. I don’t really feel like I relax anymore in the normal sense of the word.\n\nIt’s like my body is wound tight almost all day. Even if I’m just sitting at my computer or I’m between tasks, my shoulders are up and locked, and I’ll realize I’m sort of bracing, like something is about to happen. Sometimes I don’t even notice it until I let out a breath or my neck starts hurting and I’m like, okay, I’ve apparently been sitting here like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings, I’ll get up and pace around, not even going anywhere useful, just back and forth. Or if I’m at chess club, which should be quiet and focused, I’ll catch myself tapping pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there calmly feels almost impossible. It’s embarrassing because I’m aware of it, but I can’t seem to settle my body down.\n\nSo yeah, I have a lot of trouble relaxing. Even when I’m supposed to be doing nothing, I feel like I’m still on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about nine months now, give or take.\n\nIt really seemed to start when the cloud migration at work ramped up. At first I told myself it was just a busy project phase and it would calm down once that got under control, but around the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular too. So it wasn’t just a rough week or even a rough month — it kind of became this constant background thing.\n\nLooking back, I can mark it pretty clearly from then. Since that whole period started, I don’t think I’ve really gone back to feeling normal for any real stretch of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think it’s that I’ve lost interest exactly. I still know, in my head, what kinds of things I usually like or want to do.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t always feel like I can fully get into them, if that makes sense. Like if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still show up for it or start doing it, but I’m not necessarily relaxed enough to really enjoy it the way I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same as losing pleasure, or if it’s just me being distracted and tense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped caring about the things I enjoy. I just don’t feel as present with them lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not most of the day, no.

I mean, I get worn down and discouraged sometimes, especially when I feel like I can’t get a handle on things, but I wouldn’t describe it as being sad all day or waking up feeling depressed and staying that way. It’s more like I’m tense or preoccupied, and then I might feel low for a bit because I’m tired of feeling that way.

But if you mean a constant sadness that’s there for most of the day, most days… I don’t think that’s really what’s happening.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is happening in my head at the same time, all the time. My marriage, whether I’m messing things up at work or falling behind, bills, stuff around the house that I keep starting and then dropping, and then I start thinking maybe I’m just failing in general — as a husband, as a father, as the person who’s supposed to keep things together.\n\nIt’s not one specific thing, really. And I know some of it probably sounds like normal adult stuff, but it feels constant, like I can’t get any space from it.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very hard. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because I can sometimes see that I’m doing it — like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on the code in front of you,” or if I’m exercising I’ll try to just breathe and count reps or whatever — and it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like one concern catches onto another one. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. I got through school, and later college and grad school, so… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate what was just normal kid behavior from something meaningful.\n\nIf anything, the trouble now feels tied up with all the worrying and second-guessing. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\nDoctor: Even when things are relatively calm, do you still find yourself worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.\n\nLike, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. I don’t really feel like I relax anymore in the normal sense of the word.\n\nIt’s like my body is wound tight almost all day. Even if I’m just sitting at my computer or I’m between tasks, my shoulders are up and locked, and I’ll realize I’m sort of bracing, like something is about to happen. Sometimes I don’t even notice it until I let out a breath or my neck starts hurting and I’m like, okay, I’ve apparently been sitting here like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings, I’ll get up and pace around, not even going anywhere useful, just back and forth. It’s embarrassing because I’m aware of it, but I can’t seem to settle my body down.\n\nSo yeah, I have a lot of trouble relaxing. Even when I’m supposed to be doing nothing, I feel like I’m still on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. Like if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still show up for it or start doing it, but I’m not necessarily relaxed enough to really enjoy it the way I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same as losing pleasure, or if it’s just me being distracted and tense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped caring about the things I enjoy. I just don’t feel as present with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or low most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, no.\n\nI mean, I get worn down and discouraged sometimes, especially when I feel like I can’t get a handle on things, but I wouldn’t describe it as being sad all day or waking up feeling depressed and staying that way. It’s more like I’m tense or preoccupied, and then I might feel low for a bit because I’m tired of feeling that way.\n\nBut if you mean a constant sadness that’s there for most of the day, most days… I don’t think that’s really what’s happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs this worry causing problems in your daily life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it is. I wish I could say it’s just something I’m feeling internally and I’m still keeping everything together, but it’s starting to show up in places I can’t really explain away anymore.

At work, for example, my manager has started actually documenting things I’ve missed — like architectural details that I normally would catch or at least follow up on. That’s been… honestly pretty embarrassing, because my job depends on being able to think clearly and keep track of those moving parts. And it’s not like I don’t care. I care a lot. But I’ll be reviewing something and my mind is already running through ten other concerns, and then later someone points out that I missed a dependency or didn’t think through part of the design carefully enough.

And at home, it’s causing problems too. I keep starting things and then leaving them half-finished, or I’m physically there but distracted enough that it’s obvious. Then that turns into an argument, or makes an argument worse, because I’m not really present or I seem like I’m not listening. So, yes… it’s definitely affecting daily life. It’s not just in my head anymore.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is happening in my head at the same time, all the time. My marriage, whether I’m messing things up at work or falling behind, bills, stuff around the house that I keep starting and then dropping, and then I start thinking maybe I’m just failing in general — as a husband, as a father, as the person who’s supposed to keep things together.\n\nIt’s not one specific thing, really. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for what I’ve forgotten or what’s about to go wrong, and then it lands on something else before I can even deal with the first thing. I work with computers, so I’m used to juggling details, but lately it feels like I’m juggling my whole life and I’m dropping pieces everywhere. And I know some of it probably sounds like normal adult stuff, but it feels constant, like I can’t get any space from it.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very hard. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because I can sometimes see that I’m doing it — like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on the code in front of you,” or if I’m exercising I’ll try to just breathe and count reps or whatever — and it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like one concern catches onto another one. I’ll start with something small, like maybe I’m behind on a task or I didn’t explain something clearly at work, and then within a few minutes I’m twenty steps ahead. I’m imagining getting a bad review, then losing my job, then not being able to support my family, and then it becomes this whole thing where I’m the reason everything falls apart. And I know, logically, that I jumped way ahead, but knowing that doesn’t make it shut off. It feels like once it gets going, I’m just along for the ride.\n\nDoctor: Before these worries became a problem, did you already have trouble with focus or finishing tasks going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. I got through school, and later college and grad school, so… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate what was just normal kid behavior from something meaningful.\n\nIf anything, the trouble now feels tied up with all the worrying and second-guessing. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. More like overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is excessive compared with the actual problems going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.\n\nBut the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\nDoctor: Even when things are relatively calm, do you still find yourself worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.\n\nLike, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. I don’t really feel like I relax anymore in the normal sense of the word.\n\nIt’s like my body is wound tight almost all day. Even if I’m just sitting at my computer or I’m between tasks, my shoulders are up and locked, and I’ll realize I’m sort of bracing, like something is about to happen. Sometimes I don’t even notice it until I let out a breath or my neck starts hurting and I’m like, okay, I’ve apparently been sitting here like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings, I’ll get up and pace around, not even going anywhere useful, just back and forth. Or if I’m at chess club, which should be quiet and focused, I’ll catch myself tapping pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there calmly feels almost impossible. It’s embarrassing because I’m aware of it, but I can’t seem to settle my body down.\n\nSo yeah, I have a lot of trouble relaxing. Even when I’m supposed to be doing nothing, I feel like I’m still on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about nine months now, give or take.\n\nIt really seemed to start when the cloud migration at work ramped up. At first I told myself it was just a busy project phase and it would calm down once that got under control, but around the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular too. It’s more like I’m tense or preoccupied, and then I might feel low for a bit because I’m tired of feeling that way.\n\nBut if you mean a constant sadness that’s there for most of the day, most days… I don’t think that’s really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Is this worry causing problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. Honestly, no… I don’t think it would mostly go away. I mean, it would help, obviously. If work calmed down and things at home felt more stable, I’d probably have less immediate stuff to spiral about.

But the problem is it doesn’t feel like the worry is only about one situation. It’s like everything stacks up at once. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about all of it — my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father.

So even if one part improved, I think my mind would just move to the next thing. Like, okay, work is better, but what about money? Or the house? Or am I still not doing enough at home? It feels less like one stressor causing the worry and more like the worry just keeps finding things to attach itself to.

And I know, logically, that I jumped way ahead, but knowing that doesn’t make it shut off. It feels like once it gets going, I’m just along for the ride.\n\nDoctor: Before these worries became a problem, did you already have trouble with focus or finishing tasks going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. I got through school, and later college and grad school, so… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate what was just normal kid behavior from something meaningful.\n\nIf anything, the trouble now feels tied up with all the worrying and second-guessing. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. More like overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is excessive compared with the actual problems going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.\n\nBut the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\nDoctor: Even when things are relatively calm, do you still find yourself worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.\n\nLike, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. I don’t really feel like I relax anymore in the normal sense of the word.\n\nIt’s like my body is wound tight almost all day. Even if I’m just sitting at my computer or I’m between tasks, my shoulders are up and locked, and I’ll realize I’m sort of bracing, like something is about to happen. Sometimes I don’t even notice it until I let out a breath or my neck starts hurting and I’m like, okay, I’ve apparently been sitting here like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings, I’ll get up and pace around, not even going anywhere useful, just back and forth. Or if I’m at chess club, which should be quiet and focused, I’ll catch myself tapping pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there calmly feels almost impossible. It’s embarrassing because I’m aware of it, but I can’t seem to settle my body down.\n\nSo yeah, I have a lot of trouble relaxing. Even when I’m supposed to be doing nothing, I feel like I’m still on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about nine months now, give or take.\n\nIt really seemed to start when the cloud migration at work ramped up. At first I told myself it was just a busy project phase and it would calm down once that got under control, but around the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular too. So it wasn’t just a rough week or even a rough month — it kind of became this constant background thing.\n\nLooking back, I can mark it pretty clearly from then. Since that whole period started, I don’t think I’ve really gone back to feeling normal for any real stretch of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think it’s that I’ve lost interest exactly. I still know, in my head, what kinds of things I usually like or want to do.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t always feel like I can fully get into them, if that makes sense. Like if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still show up for it or start doing it, but I’m not necessarily relaxed enough to really enjoy it the way I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same as losing pleasure, or if it’s just me being distracted and tense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped caring about the things I enjoy. I just don’t feel as present with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or low most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, no.\n\nI mean, I get worn down and discouraged sometimes, especially when I feel like I can’t get a handle on things, but I wouldn’t describe it as being sad all day or waking up feeling depressed and staying that way. It’s more like I’m tense or preoccupied, and then I might feel low for a bit because I’m tired of feeling that way.\n\nBut if you mean a constant sadness that’s there for most of the day, most days… I don’t think that’s really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Is this worry causing problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I wish I could say it’s just something I’m feeling internally and I’m still keeping everything together, but it’s starting to show up in places I can’t really explain away anymore.\n\nAt work, for example, my manager has started actually documenting things I’ve missed — like architectural details that I normally would catch or at least follow up on. That’s been… honestly pretty embarrassing, because my job depends on being able to think clearly and keep track of those moving parts. And it’s not like I don’t care. I care a lot. But I’ll be reviewing something and my mind is already running through ten other concerns, and then later someone points out that I missed a dependency or didn’t think through part of the design carefully enough.\n\nAnd at home, it’s causing problems too. I keep starting things and then leaving them half-finished, or I’m physically there but distracted enough that it’s obvious. Then that turns into an argument, or makes an argument worse, because I’m not really present or I seem like I’m not listening. So, yes… it’s definitely affecting daily life. It’s not just in my head anymore.\n\nDoctor: If the work and home stress were to improve, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no… I don’t think it would mostly go away. I mean, it would help, obviously. If work calmed down and things at home felt more stable, I’d probably have less immediate stuff to spiral about.\n\nBut the problem is it doesn’t feel like the worry is only about one situation. It’s like everything stacks up at once. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about all of it — my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nSo even if one part improved, I think my mind would just move to the next thing. Like, okay, work is better, but what about money? Or the house? Or am I still not doing enough at home? It feels less like one stressor causing the worry and more like the worry just keeps finding things to attach itself to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m walking around angry all the time or anything like that.\n\nMaybe I get a little shorter with people than I’d like, but it’s hard for me to separate whether that’s irritability or just being tired and preoccupied. Like, if someone asks me something when I’m already mentally tangled up, I might answer too sharply and then later feel bad about it. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it as its own thing.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure it’s one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I mean, it would help, obviously. If work calmed down and things at home felt more stable, I’d probably have less immediate stuff to spiral about.\n\nBut the problem is it doesn’t feel like the worry is only about one situation. It’s like everything stacks up at once. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about all of it — my marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nSo even if one part improved, I think my mind would just move to the next thing. Like, okay, work is better, but what about money? Or the house? Or am I still not doing enough at home? It feels less like one stressor causing the worry and more like the worry just keeps finding things to attach itself to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, honestly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m walking around angry all the time or anything like that.\n\nMaybe I get a little shorter with people than I’d like, but it’s hard for me to separate whether that’s irritability or just being tired and preoccupied. Like, if someone asks me something when I’m already mentally tangled up, I might answer too sharply and then later feel bad about it. But I haven’t really been keeping track of it as its own thing.\n\nSo… maybe somewhat, but I’m not sure it’s one of the main things I’ve noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.

Most nights I’ll feel completely exhausted, like my body is ready to shut down, but then as soon as I’m in bed my brain starts up again. I’ll be lying there going over work risks, things I might have missed, money stuff, and then replaying something I said to my wife — like whether I came across wrong or made things worse. It’s not even productive thinking. It’s just this loop.

And even when I do finally fall asleep, I don’t stay asleep very well. I’ve been waking up around 3 or 4 in the morning, and once I’m awake, that’s basically it. My mind is already rehearsing tomorrow’s problems before the day has even started, and I can’t get back down from it. So I end up starting the day already drained.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I came in because I just can’t seem to stop worrying. It’s like everything is happening in my head at the same time, all the time. My marriage, whether I’m messing things up at work or falling behind, bills, stuff around the house that I keep starting and then dropping, and then I start thinking maybe I’m just failing in general — as a husband, as a father, as the person who’s supposed to keep things together.\n\nIt’s not one specific thing, really. It’s more like my mind keeps scanning for what I’ve forgotten or what’s about to go wrong, and then it lands on something else before I can even deal with the first thing. I work with computers, so I’m used to juggling details, but lately it feels like I’m juggling my whole life and I’m dropping pieces everywhere. And I know some of it probably sounds like normal adult stuff, but it feels constant, like I can’t get any space from it.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very hard. That’s actually one of the things that scares me, because I can sometimes see that I’m doing it — like I’ll literally tell myself, “Okay, stop, just focus on the code in front of you,” or if I’m exercising I’ll try to just breathe and count reps or whatever — and it doesn’t really stop.\n\nIt’s like one concern catches onto another one. I’ll start with something small, like maybe I’m behind on a task or I didn’t explain something clearly at work, and then within a few minutes I’m twenty steps ahead. I’m imagining getting a bad review, then losing my job, then not being able to support my family, and then it becomes this whole thing where I’m the reason everything falls apart. And I know, logically, that I jumped way ahead, but knowing that doesn’t make it shut off. It feels like once it gets going, I’m just along for the ride.\n\nDoctor: Before these worries became a problem, did you already have trouble with focus or finishing tasks going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think I had this same kind of problem going all the way back to childhood, at least not in a way that anyone flagged or that I remember as a major thing.\n\nI mean, I was probably a normal kid in terms of procrastinating sometimes or not wanting to do homework, and I’m sure I had unfinished stuff here and there, but I don’t remember it being this pattern where I just couldn’t focus or couldn’t finish anything no matter what. I got through school, and later college and grad school, so… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate what was just normal kid behavior from something meaningful.\n\nIf anything, the trouble now feels tied up with all the worrying and second-guessing. But as far as childhood, I don’t have a clear memory of having a long-standing focus problem before all this.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one single event where I can say, “That day changed everything.” It was more that things started building up, and then at some point I realized I’d been living like this for months.\n\nI’d say it’s been about nine months now. Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up — that became this constant pressure, a lot of moving parts and things that could go wrong — and at the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular. Not just one bad fight, but this pattern where it felt like there was always tension waiting for me in one place or the other.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d call it a specific event exactly. More like work got heavier, home got harder, and since then the worrying just hasn’t really let up.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from everything else. I wouldn’t say I’m… depressed in the sense of feeling sad all day or like there’s this heavy dark mood constantly, at least I don’t think so.\n\nI do feel worn down a lot, and sometimes kind of discouraged, like I’m tired of my own brain and tired of feeling like I can’t get a normal amount of peace. But when you say “down or depressed,” I’m not sure if that counts or if that’s more just being stressed and exhausted. I haven’t really been thinking of myself as depressed, exactly. More like overwhelmed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is excessive compared with the actual problems going on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s honestly why I came in. It feels excessive, even if some of the problems are real. Like, yes, there are things going on — my marriage has been tense, work has been a lot, money and bills are always kind of in the back of my head, and there are house projects I’ve started and then dropped. So it’s not like I’m worrying about absolutely nothing.\n\nBut the amount of worrying feels out of proportion. It’s like all of it is happening at once in my head, all the time. I’ll be thinking about whether I’m slipping at work, and then it turns into whether I’m going to mess something up badly, and then somehow that connects to whether I’m failing at home, or failing as a husband, or as a father. And then I’m thinking about bills, and the house, and all the things I haven’t finished, and it just keeps stacking up.\n\nSo… yeah. The problems are there, but my reaction to them feels bigger than the actual situation sometimes. I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nDoctor: Are you worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the problems at work and home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not just work and home, though those are the big obvious ones. It’s like once my brain gets going, it pulls in everything else too.\n\nI’ll start with something concrete, like whether I’m keeping up at work, and then it spreads into, “Am I slipping? Are people noticing? Am I going to drop something important?” Then I’ll come home and it’s the marriage stuff, like replaying conversations or worrying that I’m making things worse. And then suddenly I’m thinking about bills, and the house projects I keep starting and not finishing, and whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It feels like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. Even when one thing settles down for a minute, another thing just takes its place.\n\nDoctor: Even when things are relatively calm, do you still find yourself worrying a lot?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s actually one of the things that makes it feel so frustrating, because even if nothing is actively blowing up in that moment, my mind doesn’t really seem to accept that as a reason to calm down.\n\nLike, I could be sitting there and technically everything is fine — no immediate crisis, nobody’s calling me, nothing has happened right then — and I’ll still be going through this whole rotation in my head. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then just dropping, and then it turns into this bigger thing of, “Am I failing as a husband? Am I failing as a father?” It’s like my brain keeps looking for the next thing to latch onto.\n\nThat’s a big part of why I came in. It’s not just that I worry when there’s a clear problem. It’s that I can’t stop worrying about everything at once, even when things are relatively calm.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble relaxing?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Very much so. I don’t really feel like I relax anymore in the normal sense of the word.\n\nIt’s like my body is wound tight almost all day. Even if I’m just sitting at my computer or I’m between tasks, my shoulders are up and locked, and I’ll realize I’m sort of bracing, like something is about to happen. Sometimes I don’t even notice it until I let out a breath or my neck starts hurting and I’m like, okay, I’ve apparently been sitting here like I’m waiting for bad news.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings, I’ll get up and pace around, not even going anywhere useful, just back and forth. Or if I’m at chess club, which should be quiet and focused, I’ll catch myself tapping pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there calmly feels almost impossible. It’s embarrassing because I’m aware of it, but I can’t seem to settle my body down.\n\nSo yeah, I have a lot of trouble relaxing. Even when I’m supposed to be doing nothing, I feel like I’m still on alert.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been going on for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely more than six months. I’d say it’s been about nine months now, give or take.\n\nIt really seemed to start when the cloud migration at work ramped up. At first I told myself it was just a busy project phase and it would calm down once that got under control, but around the same time the arguments at home started becoming more regular too. So it wasn’t just a rough week or even a rough month — it kind of became this constant background thing.\n\nLooking back, I can mark it pretty clearly from then. Since that whole period started, I don’t think I’ve really gone back to feeling normal for any real stretch of time.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think it’s that I’ve lost interest exactly. I still know, in my head, what kinds of things I usually like or want to do.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t always feel like I can fully get into them, if that makes sense. Like if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still show up for it or start doing it, but I’m not necessarily relaxed enough to really enjoy it the way I used to. But I don’t know if that’s the same as losing pleasure, or if it’s just me being distracted and tense.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve completely stopped caring about the things I enjoy. I just don’t feel as present with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or low most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the day, no.\n\nI mean, I get worn down and discouraged sometimes, especially when I feel like I can’t get a handle on things, but I wouldn’t describe it as being sad all day or waking up feeling depressed and staying that way. It’s more like I’m tense or preoccupied, and then I might feel low for a bit because I’m tired of feeling that way.\n\nBut if you mean a constant sadness that’s there for most of the day, most days… I don’t think that’s really what’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Is this worry causing problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is.
